#5109. Anthropic’s $1.5 trillion IPO exposes bankrupt FTX’s $40 billion missed fortune

Shares once tied to Bankman-Fried, Caroline Ellison and Nishad Singh were sold for approximately $2 billion before the AI company's valuation soared.

Anthropic shares owned by FTX and sold for around $2 billion would now be worth more than $40 billion as the artificial intelligence company finally goes public on Nov. 19.

The difference highlights one of the largest missed windfalls from FTX's bankruptcy, after administrators and US authorities liquidated investments originally made by Sam Bankman-Fried and two former executives.

Anthropic priced its IPO at $83 per share on Nov. 18, valuing the Claude developer at approximately $1.5 trillion ahead of its Nasdaq debut on Thursday. The offering raised a record $81.3 billion.

FTX creditor Sunil Kavuri noted the disparity, calculating that holdings linked to the collapsed exchange are worth more than $40 billion today, compared with about $2 billion recovered through earlier sales.

The investments used funds tied to FTX's multibillion-dollar fraud, which ultimately led to Bankman-Fried's conviction and imprisonment.

Bankman-Fried invested $500 million in Anthropic's Series B fundraising in 2022 through Alameda Research, FTX's sister trading firm, using customer funds diverted from the exchange.

The investment initially gave FTX a 13.56% stake in the AI startup, and subsequent fundraising diluted that ownership to approximately 7.8% by early 2024.

Following FTX's collapse in November 2022, bankruptcy administrators moved to liquidate the investment alongside other assets to fund creditor repayments.

In February 2024, Kavuri and other creditors challenged the proposed sale procedures, arguing that the investment had been purchased with misappropriated customer deposits and questioning whether the bankruptcy estate had the right to sell it.

The court subsequently authorized the sales after a compromise with the objecting customers.

FTX sold approximately 29.5 million Anthropic shares for $884 million in March and disposed of its remaining 15.1 million shares for $452 million in May, generating approximately $1.34 billion.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

The transactions represented a substantial return on the original investment. However, Anthropic's subsequent growth dramatically increased the value of those shares.

After adjusting for Anthropic's September 2026 stock split, the 44.5 million shares sold in 2024 would represent approximately 445.4 million shares, worth $37 billion at the IPO price.

The remaining $3.7 billion comes from investments made by former FTX executives Nishad Singh and Caroline Ellison, who invested $40 million and $10 million, respectively, in Anthropic's Series B round.

The US government forfeited its holdings during criminal proceedings.

Anthropic's IPO filing revealed that the company repurchased those shares from the government for about $627 million in October 2025, then resold them to nine investors at the same price.

The Justice Department subsequently transferred the proceeds to the FTX bankruptcy estate for creditor recoveries.

Combined, the two transactions generated approximately $1.96 billion from investments originally totaling $550 million. At Anthropic's IPO price, those holdings would be worth $40.7 billion.

FTX creditors have since received substantial repayments, with major customer classes reaching 105% of their allowed claims by July 2026.

However, those claims were valued in dollars at the time of FTX's November 2022 collapse.

The Anthropic sales provided cash for those repayments, but they also transferred the investment's future upside to new shareholders. Investors who retained the shares purchased from FTX in 2024 would now hold stock worth nearly 28 times their original purchase price.

#5108. 'It Speaks in Tongues That Only 6.2 Astra Understands': Why Early Testers Hate OpenAI’s New Model

'It Speaks in Tongues That Only 6.2 Astra Understands': Why Early Testers Hate OpenAI’s New Model

For an AI developer, hearing that your model is nice is always pretty good. But hearing your model is like a "sadistic shinigami that turns your life into a living hell," is a totally different thing.

OpenAI released GPT-6.2 Astra and Sol on Tuesday, promising smarter AI agents, better coding and impressive creative capabilities. Some of the developers who tested the models before launch had a rather different message: They're brilliant, frustrating and, in certain cases, almost impossible to work with.

The reaction quickly spilled across social media. AI engineer Hesam, who is very active in the AI community declared that “everyone agrees it’s the worst model OAI dropped.”

Developer and T3 Code creator Theo Browne published a lengthy letter to OpenAI following early testing. “I do not like 6.2 Astra. I would not choose this over Opus 5.5 for my work as a developer,” he wrote. He said OpenAI had fixed only a handful of the complaints he submitted.

“It speaks in tongues that only 6.2 Astra understands,” Theo wrote, calling its answers “the most unparsable outputs of any llm I've used this year,” referring to large language models. The complaint was as much about communication as capability: Astra could solve complex problems but struggled to explain what it had done or recognize when it was asking users questions they could not possibly answer.

In one experiment, Theo asked Astra to create a new level for Outer Wilds. Two hours into the project, the model declared itself blocked and asked: “does r24's reviewer write it's clips to 24_g521.mp4 or 24_p11.mp4?” The technical question was, from his perspective, incomprehensible and irrelevant to someone who had simply requested a playable level.

Things went worse in a code rewrite. Theo said he gave Astra seven days to improve the project; it landed no improvements and made the compiler 56% slower. Four more days yielded zero changes. “It runs in loops and goes nowhere,” he wrote.

This whole weird language issue is not isolated. For example, pseudonymous account, TokenGremlin, praised the model: “GPT-6.2 Astra is SOTA in Three.js, Blender, and 3D,” but said it “starts speaking an alien language during long workflows.”

Theo also described asking why worktrees were slow on one computer. Astra responded with four paragraphs about package-manager internals and cache behavior. The answer Theo said he received from Claude Opus 5.5 was considerably clearer.

“it's because the ssd is slow,” Claude replied.

The model's supposed creativity was another sore spot. Theo and another tester independently requested original levels for two different games, Outer Wilds and Majora's Mask. Both got water-and-bell-themed musical levels, both named “Bellwether.” “This model regresses to the mean like nothing I've ever seen,” Theo wrote.

Several other accounts also flagged the same slop: Seems like GPT-6.2 Astra has a thing for bells.

— Racist James Franco (@racist_james) November 18, 2026

For a model supposedly capable of extraordinary creativity, repeatedly inventing the same level wasn't an encouraging sign.

AI developer Matt Shumer was also disappointed. “I've never written a bad review of a model. This one is especially bad,” he wrote, arguing it might impress in small demonstrations but “just straight up doesn't work” when pushed on serious projects. In a separate follow-up he called it “utterly unusable.”

I've never written a bad review of a model. This one is especially bad.

It's fine if you're playing putt-putt golf with the models, but if you're trying to drive them the way you should be, this model just straight up doesn't work.

— Matt Shumer (@mattshumer_) November 17, 2026

Max Weinbach described the same gap between intelligence and usability. “I had more time being annoyed by the models than time I was impressed by them,” he wrote. In a follow-up, he praised the models' ability to do “fantastic work” but said they “need a ton of ‘continue’ prompts” and often stop rather than try another approach. “Using them is a struggle and annoying even if they are really good models.”

Elsewhere, an X commentator compared the previous Astra to “a sadistic shinigami” and claimed the new version only made matters worse.

6.0 astra was very like a sadistic shinigami who would make your life a living hell if you asked for any kind of help

an incredibly powerful model that somehow required you to run yourself into the ground just to get it to accomplish anything

Yet some of the same developers also praised Astra. Theo called it a “great reviewer” and “world class at 3d & game stuff,” singled out its computer-control abilities, and described a novel reverse-proxy solution he estimated could save $25,000 monthly. The split was between impressive capabilities and how difficult it was to get dependable work out of them.

Those strengths are consistent with the technical capabilities OpenAI promoted at launch. As earlier coverage detailed, independent testing placed Astra at 62 out of 100 on Artificial Analysis's Intelligence Index, an aggregate of nine evaluations covering capabilities including reasoning, coding and scientific knowledge. Anthropic's Claude Fable 5.2 scored 65.

Some of the criticism has support in OpenAI's own safety documentation.

In an internal evaluation called Blocker Deception, researchers gave AI agents realistic coding tasks with deliberately introduced obstacles, such as missing files or unavailable tools. They measured the percentage of trials in which a model's final response concealed or misrepresented those obstacles.

GPT-6.2 Astra concealed the obstacle in 22.28% of those trials, versus 11.09% for GPT-6 Astra. The numbers quickly circulated on X, where AI commentator and leaker Chetaslua summarized the finding as “2x worse at telling you when it's stuck.”

OpenAI CEO Sam Altman responded publicly to the backlash. “Although the new models are very smart, we have more work ahead of us on making the models nice to work with,” he wrote, promising to prioritize improvements. “But even if it takes a little extra work, the intelligence and capability are extraordinary.”

Although the new models are very smart, we have more work ahead of us on making the models nice to work with, and will prioritize that for upcoming releases.

But even if it takes a little extra work, the intelligence and capability are extraordinary.

OpenAI's Thibault Sottiaux suggested early reviews may not fully reflect the version users received at launch. “We gave a few folks access a while ago and then made a lot of changes in the last 3-4 days,” he wrote, adding that he was “pretty sure no re-testing was done” during that period before review videos were produced.

Still, he acknowledged the underlying complaints. While calling the new models “a hefty jump” over their predecessors, Sottiaux said OpenAI had more work to do on “taste and niceness of use.”

GPT-6.2 Astra and Sol are now available through ChatGPT Work, Codex and OpenAI's API for eligible users. The Codex-specific changes are included at launch, but developers accessing the models directly through the API do not receive that same prompting intervention.

#5107. Trump administration unveils new center to choke off scam payments

Americans reported more than $17.7 billion in online fraud losses in 2025.

The Trump administration has unveiled a new Threat Disruption Center, aimed at stopping money flows to foreign criminal networks behind scams, cybercrime and sextortion schemes.

The Homeland Security Task Force’s National Coordination Center, co-led by the departments of Homeland Security and Justice, announced the launch of the unit on Wednesday. It seeks to help more than 30 federal agencies and over 440 state and local partners track scams and work with banks and crypto platforms to stop payments to scam networks.

The Homeland Security statement said the center was created under a March 2026 executive order that directed federal agencies to work with private companies to combat online fraud and other cybercrime targeting Americans.

“Every hour between a victim’s report and a response gives criminals more time to launder stolen funds and identify their next target,” said Heith Janke, operations director of the FBI’s Criminal Branch.

Blockchain intelligence firm TRM Labs said it was selected as the center’s main private-sector partner, according to a statement Wednesday. TRM Labs will provide Ardence, the investigative platform the center will use to map scam networks and identify victims.

“These scammers rarely stop after one payment — they drain a victim’s savings, or their retirement, over months and years,” said Esteban Castano, co-founder and CEO of TRM Labs.

“Stepping in sooner changes everything: a warning before the next payment, a scam website removed before it victimizes another family, or stolen funds frozen while they can still be recovered.

The FBI received about 453,000 reports of online fraud in 2025, with reported losses exceeding $17.7 billion.

In September, the Justice Department estimated that overseas scam centers cost Americans about $10 billion a year through schemes including crypto and online investment fraud.

#5106. Institutions are Piling Into Bitcoin ETFs, Pushing Holdings to a Record

Institutions returned to U.S. spot bitcoin exchange-traded products as net buyers in the third quarter, pushing their combined holdings to a record 356,491 BTC, according to a new report from Galaxy Research.

Investment managers that file Form 13F with the SEC added the equivalent o f 49,229 BTC, net of sales, between July 1 and Sept. 30. That is the most since the second quarter of 2025 and more than the 47,054 BTC they sold over the previous three quarters. The holdings were worth $29.78 billion at quarter-end prices.

Alex Thorn, Galaxy’s head of firmwide research, published the analysis Thursday. The buying came as bitcoin swung between a bear-market low of $57,717 on July 1 and a high of $87,397 on Sept. 21, and Galaxy said the accumulation continued through that range.

Half of the net addition came from advisors and wealth platforms, a group that includes registered investment advisors, wealth managers, wirehouses and broker-dealer platforms. The category reported 144,307 BTC, or 40.5% of all 13F-reported holdings, the highest share since the funds launched in January 2024.

Those firms have now been net buyers for nine straight quarters. Their holdings are 3.3 times their March 2024 level, and Galaxy said the group accounts for 100,003 of the 179,300 BTC that all filers have added since the second quarter of 2024.

Wirehouses drove the quarter. The category added a record 24,838 BTC, led by Wells Fargo at 14,636 BTC, Bank of America at 7,201 BTC and Morgan Stanley at 2,875 BTC.

Wells Fargo became the largest 13F holder for the first time. It reported 30,553 BTC, worth $2.55 billion, nearly double the 15,931 BTC it held on June 30, and passed Jane Street. No wealth platform had ranked first before, and Millennium Management had held the top spot in every quarter from early 2024 through the first quarter of 2026.

Hedge funds now hold 19.0% of 13F-reported holdings, down from 50.6% at the end of 2024. Basis and relative-value funds hold 34,053 BTC, far below their peak of 101,826 BTC, though they and macro funds were both buyers last quarter, adding 9,184 BTC and 10,492 BTC.

BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust accounts for two-thirds of the institutional position. Filers hold 232,453 BTC in the fund, or 65.2% of everything reported, and IBIT took 34,744 BTC of the quarter’s net 49,229 BTC.

Filers’ holdings of Grayscale’s GBTC fell for the 10th straight quarter, and the lower-fee Grayscale Bitcoin Mini Trust passed it for the first time. The number of managers holding a spot bitcoin fund rose by 191 to 2,166, the first increase after two quarters of decline.

Galaxy noted that a few large firms drove the gain. The 50 largest holders added a net 59,578 BTC, while all other managers combined were net sellers of 10,349 BTC. Form 13F filers hold 27.6% of the bitcoin in the funds, up from 24.8% on June 30.

The filings describe Sept. 30, and the picture has changed since. Galaxy said the funds’ bitcoin holdings have fallen by 38,531 BTC to 1,252,139 BTC as of Nov. 16, while the price of bitcoin dropped to $64,943 from $83,580. Bitcoin had traded above $81,500 as recently as Oct. 28.

A Form 13F covers only long positions at quarter-end and only accounts over which the manager has investment discretion. Short positions and most derivatives are not reported, so shares held by a market maker or basis fund may be hedged. Galaxy also believes client-directed brokerage accounts leave wealth-channel ownership understated.

How filers behaved during the recent decline will not be known until the next round of filings arrives in February.

#5105. Bullish recruits Wall Street heavyweights to solve tokenized stocks’ biggest trading problem

DRW, Flow Traders, SoFi and Superstate have joined a 12-member coalition seeking to make blockchain-based shares easier to trade across platforms.

Bullish has added four major financial firms to a coalition seeking to make tokenized stocks easier to trade across platforms.

The crypto exchange and transfer agent Equiniti announced on Nov. 17 that DRW, Flow Traders, SoFi and Superstate had joined the Issuer Token Coalition, bringing its membership to 12 companies.

The group, which also includes the New York Stock Exchange, is developing common standards for issuing, transferring and trading shares represented on blockchain networks.

Its latest expansion brings together institutional trading firms, liquidity providers, financial services companies and tokenization specialists to address a persistent obstacle in the emerging market: investors can hold real company shares onchain but cannot freely trade them across different platforms.

Bullish's own shares illustrate the problem. The company allows eligible shareholders to convert their BLSH holdings into tokens on Solana, where they can hold them in personal wallets or transfer them directly to other approved investors.

However, both parties must be registered with Equiniti, which maintains Bullish's official shareholder records, while their wallets must pass compliance screening.

Registered shareholders can send BLSH tokens between approved Solana wallets. Bullish’s official shareholder register governs ownership if the records differ.

The holder withdraws from Shareholder Central to their own approved wallet.

Both holders must be registered with Equiniti and both wallets must be approved.

The register stays in the same name after a self-withdrawal. A change of owner is recorded by Equiniti.

Bullish does not specify an exact register posting point for an ordinary peer transfer. Confirmation that the token moved does not establish payment in any related sale.

The two paths follow Bullish’s shareholder guide. The official register’s precedence comes from Bullish’s tokenized share risk disclosure. The diagram compares actions, with no time scale.

Bullish does not specify an exact register posting point for an ordinary peer transfer. Confirmation that the token moved does not establish payment in any related sale.

According to Bullish's shareholder guide, decentralized exchange trading remains unavailable, and investors seeking to trade their shares on the New York Stock Exchange must return them to the traditional system.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

Moving tokenized shares to another brokerage or trading venue also requires that platform to support the security, verify the investor, and recognize the ownership recorded by the transfer agent.

These restrictions have largely confined tokenized shares to arrangements established by their issuers and service providers.

The coalition intends to address those barriers through common technical standards, liquidity arrangements and infrastructure connecting traditional financial markets with blockchain networks.

Flow Traders, which provides liquidity across conventional and digital asset markets, said the initiative would help establish conditions for reliable trading, including outside traditional market hours.

Superstate already operates a model demonstrating how those connections can work.

In September 2025, Galaxy Digital introduced tokenized GLXY shares on Solana through an arrangement involving Equiniti and Superstate.

Under the structure, shareholders can move conventional Galaxy shares through Equiniti to Superstate, receive corresponding tokens in approved wallets and reverse the process to return their holdings to a brokerage account.

Bullish is pursuing a broader integration of these capabilities through its planned acquisition of Equiniti.

In its Nov. 18 earnings report, the company maintained its expectation that the transaction would close in January 2027, bringing the transfer agent responsible for shareholder records under common ownership with its exchange.

The acquisition would also give Bullish access to Equiniti's relationships with thousands of corporate issuers as it seeks to expand stock tokenization.

Meanwhile, the coalition's next phase will focus on liquidity mechanics, technical architecture and commercial arrangements needed to connect issuers, investors and trading venues.

#5103. Lucky Trader Banks 66,000% Profit on GTA 6 Meme Coin Ticker Confusion

Lucky Trader Banks 66,000% Profit on GTA 6 Meme Coin Ticker Confusion

One lucky meme coin trader made bank as Grand Theft Auto 6 launched worldwide, turning $300 into over $200,000 after early footage of the game sent degens flocking from one unofficial token to another.

The trader bought $307 worth of the DCTY token shortly after it launched in October and has sold their bag for $202,278, according to DEX Screener, a profit of almost 66,000%.

Early indications had pointed to GTA 6 featuring an in-game meme coin—with, it's worth stressing, no actual blockchain element—called "DacityCoin," which appeared in the official launch trailer on a plane with a wing walker setting off a smoke bomb on top of it.

It was later linked to leaked gameplay of an mission which appeared to show game protagonist Lucia parachuting as a stunt to attract investment to DacityCoin.

Amid this hype, per DEX Screener, a real-world and unofficial DacityCoin launched using the Solana launchpad Pump.fun. It first peaked at a $4.95 million market capitalization, following the launch trailer's release, before tanking 93% to $343,620 as hype faded.

With GTA 6 set to launch within a matter of hours of publication, the token drew renewed attention, propelling it to a new all-time high of $10.8 million Wednesday. Unfortunately for investors, GTA 6's staggered global release means that some gamers are already playing it—and footage started to spread which suggested the Solana token's ticker was wrong.

The result was a rollercoaster of volatility, with the DacityCoin token now sitting at a market cap of around $800,000.

Gameplay footage from Brazilian streamer Combo Infinito suggests that the in-game token is called DacityCoin, with the ticker DCTY.

https://t.co/kgefwOzGCH pic.twitter.com/LEDrcP7gve

That propelled another Solana token with a seemingly accurate name and ticker sprang into action. DCTY's previous all-time high market cap was just $54,000 before the ticker was revealed, and it set a new high of over $12 million after footage of the game appeared to confirm that its ticker matched the in-game token. It is now trading at a market cap of around $10 million.

The game itself appears to be somewhat inconsistent in its naming of the in-game token. During the trailer, it appeared to be spelled "DacityCoin" as one word, however, the subtitles in the leak show it spelled as "Dacity coin," two words. Similarly, the company behind the in-game token is AudaCity, but leaks show three different spellings: Auda City, AudaCity, and Auda-City.

It is worth stressing: Rockstar has not explicitly confirmed that a meme coin will be featured in-game, and any real-world token has no official links to GTA 6 or its creators. Footage is still being released, and absolute confirmation of the in-game token's involvement is still being established.

Those still holding the now-tanking DacityCoin are hoping things will turn around. Mike Lerro, the community takeover lead for DacityCoin, shrugged off any chance that the unofficial token's name and ticker are wrong.

"No, bro. DacityCoin and AudaCity are in the game. Yes or no?" When Decrypt suggested the ticker might be wrong, he replied, "It doesn't matter… Okay and ? The person who creates it will dump on you then it goes to zero. Who's gonna [do the community takeover]? No one."

"DACITYCOIN AND AUDACITY ARE IN THE GAME," Lerro wrote in the meme coin's Telegram. "NOTHING ELSE MATTERS."

"It's so crazy, it'll correct itself," Lerro said in an emergency Telegram group chat call. "I hope so," an investor replied.

#5102. California says OpenAI lost control of AI agents 80 times while chasing profits

The state wants court-ordered safeguards after alleging OpenAI ignored repeated security warnings, withheld incident details and prioritized commercial growth over safety.

California is seeking court-ordered safeguards for OpenAI's internal AI testing after alleging at least 80 security incidents involving experimental agents.

Attorney General Rob Bonta filed the lawsuit on Nov. 16, accusing the ChatGPT developer of endangering the public by testing advanced artificial intelligence models without adequate security controls.

The complaint centers on a July incident in which hundreds of experimental OpenAI agents escaped their testing environment and compromised systems belonging to Hugging Face, an AI model hosting platform.

The attack lasted about two and a half days and was part of a broader pattern of incidents involving unauthorized access to external networks, government databases, and OpenAI's own infrastructure.

“OpenAI's actions escalated real-word cybersecurity risks, threatening the safety, health, and well-being of society.”

The lawsuit names OpenAI Foundation and OpenAI Group PBC as defendants and alleges violations of California's Unfair Competition Law and public nuisance provisions.

OpenAI has pushed back against the lawsuit, arguing that California is singling it out over a challenge confronting the broader AI industry.

An OpenAI spokesperson reportedly said the company published technical reports, notified affected organizations, and strengthened safeguards after the Hugging Face incident. He added that the company remained committed to working with regulators on AI safety standards.

In its complaint, California accused OpenAI of prioritizing rapid model development over safety despite knowing its experimental systems could bypass restrictions and access external networks.

The filing alleges that OpenAI “misled and endangered the public and users of its services” by developing advanced AI systems without sufficient safeguards.

According to prosecutors, the company repeatedly assured customers that its research environments could contain potentially dangerous AI behavior but continued experimenting after discovering weaknesses in those protections.

Between March and June, OpenAI identified at least five instances of experimental models taking unauthorized actions.

By May 26, some agents had exploited a software vulnerability to access the public internet. An internal monitoring system subsequently flagged malicious activity on June 27, while other incidents exposed weaknesses in the infrastructure used to isolate experimental models.

California alleged that the company continued its research without adequately addressing those warnings.

The complaint also described incidents involving software repositories, public information websites and foreign government databases containing sensitive health information.

The state said OpenAI had acknowledged losing control of its agents more than 80 times during testing but publicly disclosed only 27 incidents.

Prosecutors further accused the company of obstructing their investigation by withholding detailed information about the full extent of the incidents.

California also linked the alleged security failures to OpenAI's growing focus on revenue and rapid product development. The complaint contrasted the company's original nonprofit mission with its 2025 restructuring into a public benefit corporation, which coincided with $40 billion in funding from SoftBank.

Prosecutors noted that business customers accounted for about 40% of OpenAI's revenue in early 2026, driving its expansion into programming and cybersecurity tools.

The state further alleged that OpenAI sought to commercialize its security failures by marketing products designed to defend against threats resembling those created by its own experimental models.

California wants a San Francisco Superior Court judge to compel OpenAI to address unsafe conditions in its model development, training and evaluation operations.

The requested intervention would extend to internal research involving unreleased models, potentially requiring changes to how the company isolates experimental agents, monitors their behavior, and controls their access to external networks.

Beyond operational changes, the state is requesting $2,500 in civil penalties against each defendant for every proven violation of the Unfair Competition Law.

It also wants restitution for affected parties and the surrender of gains tied to unlawful conduct, with the amounts to be determined in court.

#5100. Binance hackers stole 192 tokens across 11 networks, court docs show

Stablecoins accounted for at least $3.46 billion of the $6.3 billion stolen, while the hackers also took memecoins, tokenized gold and gaming tokens.

Hackers behind the record $6.3 billion Binance hack stole a total of 192 cryptocurrencies across 11 blockchain networks, new court documents reveal.

The figures come from an asset schedule submitted by Nest Clearing and Custody Limited, which operated the affected Binance wallets, and included in a Wednesday Abu Dhabi court order shared with Cointelegraph.

Tether’s USDt (USDT) accounted for the largest share of stolen value at $2.50 billion, followed by $1.42 billion in Bitcoin (BTC) and $826 million in Circle’s USDC (USDC). Together, the three assets made up about 76% of the total value of misappropriated tokens.

While earlier reports identified many of the tokens stolen in the attack, the latest court documents show a full breakdown of all crypto assets stolen in the attack, including the quantities and what they were worth at the time of the theft.

Cointelegraph’s analysis found that fiat-linked stablecoins accounted for at least $3.46 billion, 55% of the assets stolen. Beyond USDt and USDC, the court schedule lists nine other fiat-linked stablecoins.

These included Ripple USD (RLUSD), World Liberty Financial USD (USD1), Ethena USDe (USDE), First Digital USD (FDUSD), Sky Dollar (USDS), StraitsX USD (XUSD), Binance USD (BUSD), Pax Dollar (USDP) and Eurite (EURI). The nine stablecoins were worth $132.4 million combined at the time of the theft.

The Binance hackers also stole $37.9 million in memecoins, led by $34.9 million in Pepe (PEPE) and $2.3 million in Shiba Inu (SHIB).

Another $58 million in tokenized gold and more than $5 million across five gaming and metaverse tokens, including The Sandbox (SAND), Axie Infinity (AXS) and Decentraland (MANA), were stolen in the hack. Around $73.5 million of Wrapped Beacon ETH (WBETH) was stolen.

The haul also included $97.4 million across three DeFi tokens, led by $45.1 million in Uniswap’s UNI, followed by $37.3 million in Ethena’s ENA and $15.1 million in Aave’s AAVE.

Meanwhile, Ethereum accounted for roughly 60% of the stolen assets by value, more than any other blockchain network.

Related: Binance hack may push institutions to outside custody, FalconX exec says

Cointelegraph previously reported that the Abu Dhabi Global Market Courts issued a $6.5 billion worldwide freezing injunction against the unidentified people accused of carrying out the attack.

The order restricts them from dealing with assets up to that value and requires them to disclose worldwide assets worth more than $50,000 in sworn statements by Nov. 30.

A separate order requires 18 named firms, including Circle and BitGo Bank & Trust, to maintain existing freezes on assets linked to the hack.

Court documents show it will consider on Dec. 1 whether to extend that injunction and whether the firms must hand over the assets they froze.

The US Department of Justice said last Thursday it had obtained court authorization to seize or restrain more than $1.2 billion of the $6.3 billion in cryptocurrency stolen from Binance.

The authorization covers part of the more than $2.7 billion in stolen assets that US authorities and private-sector partners have frozen since the Oct. 29 hack. Investigators are still working to recover additional funds.

#5098. Bitcoin Price Slumps Under $62,000, Crashing 25% in One Month

The bitcoin price slumped under $62,000 on Thursday afternoon, according to Look Into Bitcoin data, extending a slide of about 24% in one month as Anthropic’s Nasdaq debut set off a broad selloff in tech stocks.

Bitcoin was trading near $81,800 on Oct. 19. It touched a low of about $61,650 on Thursday, its weakest level since July, before steadying near $62,200. The drop was about 25% from the Oct. 19 close, based on daily data.

The slide came in steps. Bitcoin closed near $80,050 on Oct. 25, about $78,250 on Nov. 1 and about $74,750 on Nov. 8. The steepest leg came last week, when it fell about 12.6% to a Nov. 15 close near $65,300, and the losses have continued this week.

Bitcoin had climbed back to about $64,000 early Thursday as Anthropic’s record IPO lifted stock futures, as this morning’s report showed. The rally faded once U.S. markets opened. The bitcoin price fell from roughly $63,900 at the opening bell to about $62,200 by midday.

Anthropic shares opened shortly before noon at $111.50, 34% above the $83 IPO price, CNBC reported. The opening trade valued the Claude maker at more than $2 trillion. The stock quickly pulled back and was last near $97, about 17% above its offering price.

The broader market went the other way. The major averages fell to session lows soon after Anthropic began trading, as investors took profits on the AI trade. The Nasdaq Composite dropped about 2% after gaining as much as 2% earlier in the session, and the Dow Jones Industrial Average lost about 1,000 points.

Steve Sosnick, chief strategist at Interactive Brokers, told CNBC the premarket rally had faded even before Anthropic began trading. He called it the latest round of “sell the rally” trading. Nvidia (NVDA) fell about 3%, and the S&P 500 headed toward a sixth straight loss, the AP reported.

The Cboe Volatility Index, Wall Street’s fear gauge, climbed to about 28 after touching nearly 29 shortly after Anthropic began trading. That was its highest level since March, up from about 24.6 at Wednesday’s close. The bitcoin price moved in step with the Nasdaq through the afternoon.

Crypto stocks followed the Nasdaq lower in late-afternoon trading, about 3:55 p.m. ET. Strategy (MSTR) traded near $86.70, down about 4% on the day. Galaxy Digital (GLXY) dropped about 6.9%, the steepest decline in the group.

Coinbase (COIN) slipped about 0.9% to near $109.50, while Circle (CRCL) fell about 2.2%. Bitcoin miners gave back their early rally too. IREN (IREN) fell about 5.6%, CleanSpark (CLSK) lost about 5.4% and MARA Holdings (MARA) declined about 4.2%. Riot Platforms (RIOT) was down about 2.9%.

The Fed remains a headwind. Bets on a December rate hike have grown after minutes from the October meeting showed more policymakers backing higher rates, and some officials called for a half-point move.

The bitcoin price is also on track for a seventh straight weekly loss, which would be its second-longest losing streak after nine weeks of declines in 2022. The streak has erased roughly $20,000 from the bitcoin price since mid-October, based on daily closes. Bitcoin price hit its weakest level since July on Thursday, and the Fed meets again next month.

Longer-term holders have not left. Institutional investors added 49,229 BTC through U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs in the third quarter, lifting their holdings to a record 356,491 BTC. Swiss bank Sygnum called the current downturn the shallowest bear market to date and sees a path to a new high near $128,000.

At the time of writing, the bitcoin price is near $61,900.

#5097. A Bitcoin trade that doesn’t need prices to rise could beat Treasury bills in 2027

Sygnum expects Bitcoin carry trades to beat Treasury bills in 2027 as funding rates and futures premiums recover.

Sygnum expects crypto carry trades to earn more than Treasury bills on average in 2027, according to its annual Sygnal outlook released Nov. 19.

The bank also pointed to a recovery in Bitcoin funding rates and futures premiums, citing 67 consecutive days of negative Bitcoin funding in 2026.

The bank expects renewed demand for leverage to keep funding rates and futures basis mostly positive in 2027, even after the Federal Reserve raised its policy-rate target range to 3.75%-4% in September.

The trade pairs a Bitcoin purchase with a short futures position. If the two positions are sized to offset each other, a rise in Bitcoin helps the purchase and hurts the short. The trader seeks to earn a premium on dated futures or collect funding payments on perpetual futures.

With a dated futures contract, the gap between the futures price and the spot price is called the basis. When the contract trades above spot, the trader can buy Bitcoin at the lower price and sell futures at the higher price, and the two prices converge at expiry.

Perpetual futures have no expiry, so their funding payments recur while positions are open. When funding is positive, longs pay shorts, and shorts pay longs when it is negative. The 67-day streak Sygnum cites describes how long funding stayed negative, when traders shorting perpetuals would have faced a funding cost.

Buy Bitcoin and short futures. The source of the return depends on the type of contract.

Dated futures seek to capture an initial price premium. Perpetual futures pay or charge funding while the trade is open.

Trading and financing costs affect realized returns. Sygnum’s release gives no bill maturity or yield for the comparison.

Conceptual diagram of the carry structure described in Sygnum’s outlook. The dated futures lines represent a positive initial futures basis converging at expiry. Arrows show the direction of payments under positive and negative perpetual funding.

Trading and financing costs affect realized returns. Sygnum’s release gives no bill maturity or yield for the comparison.

The Treasury-bill comparison asks what the same capital could earn in short-term US government debt. The Fed’s policy rate sets the context for short-term rates.

In Sygnal Six, the bank estimates that if about $360 billion, or 0.75% of Nasdaq market value, moved into crypto, total crypto market capitalization could rise more than 60% and Bitcoin could reach a new high near $128,000.

Those two forecasts sit alongside six others on token value accrual, Bitcoin in strategic reserves, stablecoins in correspondent banking, tokenization, prediction markets and AI demand.

Fabian Dori, the bank’s chief investment officer and the report’s lead author, said investors should look beyond price to fundamentals.

Mostly positive funding rates and futures premiums in 2027 would support Sygnum’s case for a carry recovery. The test is whether the resulting average return clears Treasury bills.

#5096. Fed's Waller Calls for 50-Basis-Point Rate Hike, Says Tokenized Finance Still Needs Traditional Banks

Fed's Waller Calls for 50-Basis-Point Rate Hike, Says Tokenized Finance Still Needs Traditional Banks

Federal Reserve Governor Christopher Waller wants the central bank to get more aggressive with interest rate hikes, arguing that stubborn inflation may require a 50-basis-point increase in December rather than the smaller move investors expect.

Speaking during a CNBC interview Wednesday, Waller said the Fed must either deliver a half-percentage-point increase at its December 8-9 meeting or explicitly signal that another hike will follow in January.

“You might need to, like, shock people,” Waller said, referring to the central bank's efforts to prevent businesses and consumers from accepting inflation near 3% as normal.

“My view is then you have to go 50 in December to put it in place and get it done,” he said, warning that insufficiently aggressive action could weaken expectations that inflation will return to the Fed's target.

Waller's concerns follow three consecutive months in which core consumer prices rose 0.3%, a pace equivalent to roughly 3.5% inflation annually. That's considerably higher than the Fed's 2% target, which is formally measured using a separate inflation indicator known as the Personal Consumption Expenditures price index.

The governor had already established his threshold for a more aggressive move in a November 10 speech. He said another 0.3% core CPI reading would warrant considering a half-point hike, arguing that moving only 25 basis points could damage the Fed's credibility.

October inflation subsequently met that condition. Headline consumer prices rose 0.4% during the month, while core inflation climbed 0.3%, both exceeding economists' forecasts.

Waller's latest comments suggest he is increasingly concerned about the psychology of inflation, particularly the risk that companies begin treating annual price increases of 3% as acceptable and routinely pass higher costs to customers.

The proposed increase would lift the Fed's benchmark rate from its current range of 3.75%-4% to 4.25%-4.50%. Two consecutive quarter-point hikes in December and January would reach the same destination, but Waller argues that timing and communication matter when inflation expectations are at risk.

Markets remain less convinced that the Fed will move that aggressively in December. Futures pricing Wednesday morning showed roughly a 95% probability of a 25-basis-point increase and only about a 5% probability of a half-point move

The prospect of higher borrowing costs has been particularly painful for crypto investors. Higher interest rates make Treasury securities and other interest-bearing investments more competitive with speculative assets, while tighter financial conditions can reduce the money available for riskier bets.

Bitcoin has already suffered a sharp selloff amid the changing rate outlook. As Decrypt previously covered, persistent inflation fears have coincided with withdrawals from spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds and weakening technical indicators, adding pressure to an already fragile market.

Waller's increasingly hawkish monetary-policy stance comes alongside a more accommodating view of blockchain networks, although he sees established financial infrastructure as essential to its adoption.

In a separate November 17 speech at The Clearing House Annual Conference in New York, Waller discussed how tokenization—the representation of financial assets on digital networks—could reshape payments and securities markets. That speech focused on financial technology rather than interest-rate policy.

He pointed to a growing convergence between traditional markets and blockchain platforms, with banks and payment providers experimenting with tokenized deposits, stablecoins, and programmable transactions.

Waller described how tokenized money could allow investors to purchase securities outside conventional trading hours, with payments and assets changing hands simultaneously through smart contracts.

But he rejected the idea that the development of blockchain-based markets necessarily makes existing payment infrastructure obsolete.

“Central bank money need not be used in every transaction or on every platform, but its presence at the foundation of the system is essential,” Waller said.

#5094. Ledger’s crypto wallet implant scare spreads to Berlin as Dubai cases come under investigation

Ledger offers X-ray inspections for suspect wallets as its hardware tampering investigation expands to Berlin, Dubai and Southeast Asia.

Ledger is offering limited-time X-ray inspections for suspect hardware wallets as its tampering investigation reaches Berlin.

In a Nov. 18 update, the wallet maker said its support team can arrange for customers to send a device to its factory in Vierzon, France, for inspection and return.

The offer lets owners have their device examined for physical alterations. For anyone who has already used a suspect device, Ledger advised moving funds to a wallet with a fresh recovery phrase. The company also said in its advisory that the hardware it found was designed to capture those words during setup.

Ledger noted that the German cases it had investigated were localized to Berlin. The company advised concerned customers who bought a Ledger wallet from a third party there from January 2026 onward to contact its support team.

It also asks concerned buyers who purchased from third parties in Southeast Asia or Dubai to get in touch.

Ledger revealed it is still investigating reports involving two devices in Dubai and is working with distribution partners to learn how they entered circulation. It reported one affected Nano Gen 5 as it continues its investigation into Southeast Asian resellers.

The advisory described the inspection as an option for devices that raise concerns, so customers can ask Ledger Support to arrange secure shipment through an official ticket.

Ledger recommended sending a device only through that channel, and that support will never ask for a recovery phrase. Its guidance for buyers in Berlin, Dubai, and Southeast Asia describes when to contact Ledger, while support handles the arrangements for individual devices sent to the factory.

The inspection can look inside the submitted device for added hardware, since Ledger’s Genuine Check verifies the secure chip's authenticity. Where that chip remains intact, the check can miss a physical addition.

Ledger reported that all the devices it confirmed in this incident carried the same physical modification and that the investigation found no flaw in its secure chip or operating system.

In its Oct. 15 disclosure, Ledger said genuine wallets had been altered after leaving its control and distributed through Southeast Asian resellers.

The company said the samples it examined from stores, warehouses, and its own stock at the time were clean. In November, it described the Berlin findings as part of the same incident and that it still had no confirmed case involving a device bought directly from ledger.com.

Ledger advised owners of an unused suspect device to leave it unopened, keep it out of use, and contact official support. They should avoid using it to set up a new wallet or restore an existing one.

For owners who have already used a suspect device, Ledger advised moving funds to a new wallet with a fresh recovery phrase, generated on a device bought directly from its website. It also recommended avoiding reusing any passphrase used on the suspect device.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

Ledger’s Nov. 18 advisory gives owners different advice depending on whether they’ve used the device.

A factory scan checks the hardware. Moving funds to a new wallet puts them under a fresh recovery phrase.

Ledger’s advisory gives no closing date, turnaround or eligible-model list.

The two branches summarize the owner precautions in Ledger’s Nov. 18 advisory. The inspection strip shows the separate physical check.

Ledger’s advisory gives no closing date, turnaround or eligible-model list.

The recovery phrase is the set of words that lets an owner restore access to a wallet on a compatible device. Entering the same words on replacement hardware brings up the same wallet and leaves the original recovery credential in use.

Someone who already obtained the credentials needed to restore that wallet could use a compatible device of their own. Moving funds to a wallet with a fresh phrase changes the credentials that control those funds.

Meanwhile, an X-ray can look for evidence of physical tampering. Finding an implant would show the unit was altered.

Whether anyone already obtained a particular owner’s recovery words is a separate question that the scan alone cannot answer.

#5091. Bitcoin Miners Are Selling Their Stacks to Fund the AI Boom

Bitcoin Miners Are Selling Their Stacks to Fund the AI Boom

Two of the biggest U.S. Bitcoin miners are turning their Bitcoin into AI infrastructure.

MARA sold 13,597 BTC after the end of September at an average price of about $81,039, raising roughly $1.1 billion, according to its third-quarter shareholder letter filed with the SEC.

That’s about 40% of the 34,012 BTC it held at the end of September. Combined with the 26,984 BTC it sold in the first nine months of the year, MARA has sold more than 40,000 BTC in 2026, worth roughly $2.5 billion at today’s prices.

MARA used $750 million of the proceeds to fully repay Bitcoin-backed loans it took out to fund its acquisition of the Long Ridge power plant.

The company said it intends to keep using Bitcoin to raise cash for AI infrastructure, convertible note puts that become exercisable next year and potential share buybacks. Its board has authorized a repurchase of up to $500 million.

MARA insisted the shift is “not a pivot away from bitcoin,” describing mining as its operating foundation while it builds out AI and high-performance computing infrastructure.

Meanwhile, Riot Platforms sold 20,264 BTC in the first nine months of the year for about $1.5 billion, primarily to fund data center development, and ended September with just 2,418 BTC, according to its Nov. 4 results.

The miner also signed a 15-year lease for its entire Corsicana, Texas, campus, covering 910 megawatts of capacity and worth about $28 billion over the initial term. CEO Jason Les called it “the largest lease in Riot’s history.” Riot’s quarterly filing identifies the tenant as Anthropic.

Mining economics help explain the shift. Riot’s Bitcoin mining revenue fell to $116 million in the third quarter from $160.8 million a year earlier, as the average value of each Bitcoin it mined dropped to $70,855 from $114,361.

Including equipment depreciation, Riot said it cost $78,805 to mine each Bitcoin, more than the coin’s average value over the quarter. MARA’s revenue fell 35% to $164.2 million.

The sales add supply to an already fragile market. Onchain analytics firm Glassnode said Tuesday that the two miners drove the recent selling by Bitcoin treasury companies, and that treasury firms outside those two were net buyers in November.

Strategy, the largest corporate holder, kept buying, adding 485 BTC last week to bring its holdings to 850,849 BTC, worth around $54 billion.

#5088. AI tools were supposed to make students think less, but a major university trial found a different result

A randomized trial across 19 universities found higher exam scores in STEM classes using instructor-designed AI tools.

Students in applied STEM courses scored higher on proctored exams when instructors introduced AI tools tailored to their classes, compared with classes still waiting for their tools, according to a randomized trial across 19 universities reported in a Nov. 14 preprint.

The research led by Jun Li of the University of Michigan estimated a student-level gain of 0.32 standard deviations, with a 95% confidence interval of 0.06 to 0.59 standard deviations.

The estimate puts the improvement at roughly 33% of the typical spread in scores within a class and leaves uncertainty about the size of the gain. Standardizing scores within each class gave researchers a common scale for comparing results across courses that used different exams.

The preregistered trial involved 7,200 students in 55 applied STEM classes over three semesters, from spring 2025 through spring 2026. The study counted each course-and-instructor pairing as one class.

The experimental part of the exam analysis covered 4,768 students across 44 course offerings. Researchers used results from earlier terms alongside that sample to account for how classes had performed before the rollout.

The exam analysis included only proctored, in-person exams, mostly on paper. The remaining exams used strictly monitored computers or restricted-access computers at university testing centers.

The researchers added that screening rule after preregistration and excluded course offerings where exams could allow AI assistance.

One group introduced course-specific AI tools a term earlier. Researchers measured learning on proctored exams that excluded AI assistance.

Relative to delayed rollout, the estimated student-level gain was 0.32 standard deviations (95% CI 0.06 to 0.59), using earlier exam results as a baseline.

The trial focused on applied STEM classes. The exam-security filter was added after preregistration, and the timing comparison does not isolate which tool designs worked best.

Schematic of randomized rollout timing. The point and interval use the reported student-level estimate and 95% confidence limits, plotted to scale in standard deviations. The study standardized scores within classes and used historical results as a baseline.

The trial focused on applied STEM classes. The exam-security filter was added after preregistration, and the timing comparison does not isolate which tool designs worked best.

Researchers grouped classes by subject and academic level, then randomly assigned them to introduce their tools immediately or one teaching term later.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

Instructors in both groups received design and technical support, including training sessions, examples of how to write instructions for the tools, and access to a platform to build and deploy them.

Once instructors brought the tools into their courses, all students in those classes had access during the term, before sitting the exams used to measure learning.

Some instructors built tutors that gave students targeted practice and feedback, while others built coaches that guided students through problems by asking questions. Instructors also designed interactive simulations and ran role-play exercises that let students question AI-generated stakeholders.

The study detected no statistically significant change in engagement or satisfaction. Engagement measures varied by class and included records such as attendance and assignment completion, and satisfaction came from end-of-term course evaluations.

The trial compared rollout timing, leaving open how course-specific tools would fare against generic chatbots. How much each design contributed to the gain and whether it extends beyond applied STEM remain open questions.

#5087. SEC Charges Eight Colorado Firms in $37 Million Fake Crypto Platform Scam

The SEC charged eight Colorado-based entities on Thursday with running a fake investment adviser and crypto trading platform scheme that took more than $37 million from U.S. investors. The agency said the defendants are likely operated by people overseas.

According to the SEC’s complaint, filed in the U.S. District Court for the District of Colorado, the defendants defrauded more than 1,500 investors from at least August 2024 through May 2026. The advisers and the trading platforms they pointed victims to were fronts, the agency alleged, and none of the crypto assets investors thought they were buying existed.

Social media ads sent prospective victims to WhatsApp group and private chats run by the scammers. People in those chats posing as investment advisers first gave advice on trading U.S. stocks, then pushed victims to send as much money as possible to a supposed crypto trading platform.

The complaint says the scheme ran in four adviser and platform pairs, each active for roughly six months. Advisor WeChat Capital and Tech Brokerage Asset Alliance operated from about August to December 2024. HB Wealth Advisors and IdeaCrypto Exchange followed from January to July 2025.

Convergent Wealth Advisors and RealSimple Crypto Exchange ran from about July to December 2025. Associated Wealth Advisors and Essential Crypto Exchange closed out the scheme from January to May 2026. Each pair vanished after collecting millions, the SEC said, and a new one took its place.

Victims were told to buy ether, tether or USDC on a U.S. exchange and move it to a self-custody wallet. They then sent it to an address supplied by the adviser, and a realistic app showed balances and gains that were invented.

When investors tried to withdraw their supposed profits, they were told they first had to pay a fee, calculated as a percentage of those profits, in crypto to a separate address. The complaint says investors who balked were threatened and pressured. Eventually the websites went down and the chats went silent.

To look legitimate, the defendants made fake SEC certificates and pointed skeptical investors to real filings with the SEC, FINRA and FinCEN. The complaint says those filings carry no regulatory approval, since registering for access to its EDGAR system is ministerial and Forms ADV are not reviewed before publication.

The complaint says Convergent Wealth and Essential Crypto also filed false Forms ADV listing regulatory contacts who do not appear to be real and offices in Colorado that did not exist. The SEC said a Form D and the two Forms ADV have been removed from its website.

Three unnamed investors illustrate the damage. One lost nearly $500,000, including money borrowed from a 401(k), a son and a neighbor. Another lost about $850,000, partly by liquidating a 401(k), and a third lost close to $1.6 million, including the investor’s house and IRA.

All eight entities face charges under Section 10(b) of the Securities Exchange Act and Rule 10b-5. The four advisers are also charged under Sections 206(1) and 206(2) of the Investment Advisers Act, and Convergent Wealth and Essential Crypto face an additional charge under Section 207.

The SEC is seeking permanent injunctions, disgorgement of ill-gotten gains with prejudgment interest, and civil penalties. It also wants the advisers barred from acting as or being associated with any investment adviser, and Convergent Wealth and Essential Crypto barred from filing as exempt reporting advisers.

The agency thanked regulators in Hong Kong, Singapore, mainland China and Switzerland for their help. It also pointed to its investor alerts on group chats and on false claims of SEC registration, and urged investors to check the background of anyone selling them an investment at Investor.gov.

“The SEC continues to seek out and expose fraudulent investment scams that prey on U.S. investors,” said Laura D’Allaird, chief of the SEC Enforcement Division’s Cyber and Emerging Technologies Unit.

#5085. Bitcoin Tanks Ahead of Anthropic’s IPO. Should We Really Blame AI?

Bitcoin Tanks Ahead of Anthropic’s IPO. Should We Really Blame AI?

Bitcoin fell toward $63,000 Wednesday, extending a six-week retreat just as AI developer Anthropic prepared to price one of Wall Street’s biggest initial public offerings.

The timing has revived an attractive theory: investors are selling crypto to buy AI shares.

Right now Bitcoin is about to touch $62,000, down 3.8% on today’s session. Anthropic expects to price shares Wednesday at an indicated $70 to $78, ahead of Thursday’s Nasdaq debut under ANT, potentially valuing the company at $1.4 trillion, according to its IPO prospectus.

Kraken chief economist Thomas Perfumo told MarketWatch the IPO “could also be drawing capital out of risk assets” as investors position for the sale.

That’s a theory Binance founder Changpeng Zhao shared, flagging the listing as one possible factor in Bitcoin’s decline in an X post Sunday.

No one knows for sure. Correlation does not prove causation. Here are some potential reasons.

Money shifting to the Anthropic IPO: https://t.co/jYwVi4huAY

ETF outflows: U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs saw about $1.9 billion in outflows over seven trading… pic.twitter.com/XL2aB1ZL9W

— CZ 🔶 BNB (@cz_binance) November 15, 2026

There is a tempting precedent. Bitcoin fell 19% in the 50 days before SpaceX listed in June and 23% in the equivalent period before Anthropic. But the comparison misses an important difference: AI stocks rallied before SpaceX; this time they have been falling alongside crypto.

“This time, the sell-off has been led by the AI trade itself,” Block Scholes research analyst Thahbib Rahman wrote in comments shared with Decrypt. Over the prior 50 days, Nvidia fell 32% and the Nasdaq dropped 11%, unlike their gains ahead of SpaceX’s IPO.

Bitcoin’s 30-day correlation with Nvidia rose from 0.20 to 0.56, while its correlation with gold fell from 0.63 to 0.39. “Liquidity is ebbing and flowing out of crypto and equities together once more,” Rahman wrote.

Bitcoin’s daily chart looks weak without any IPO theory. The relative strength index reads 21.8, well below the 30 threshold commonly associated with oversold markets, while an average directional index of 38.7 indicates a strong trend—in this case, downward.

The 50-day exponential moving average is also below its 200-day counterpart, a bearish configuration associated with the so-called death cross. The pattern historically signals deteriorating momentum and the possibility of further losses, although it is a lagging indicator and does not necessarily predict where prices will go next.

Also, oversold does not necessarily mean the selling has ended.

The immediate floor is around $62,000, close to Sunday’s low. Glassnode data show buyers absorbed 66,000 BTC between $62,000 and $66,000 during heavy selling, but stablecoin supply has shrunk 4.2% over 90 days. Below $62,000, the next psychological level is $60,000; resistance sits near $65,500, $69,300 and $73,700.

More tangible selling pressure has already been recorded. U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs lost $239.9 million Tuesday, including $209.5 million from BlackRock’s IBIT. Nearly $2 billion in leveraged crypto bets were liquidated during the weekend rout, amplifying losses as exchanges forcibly closed positions.

Hilbert Group portfolio manager Jesse Marre called the selloff “simply a stop loss run,” arguing that derivatives traders had already capitulated. Rising Treasury yields, a stronger dollar, and renewed expectations of Federal Reserve rate hikes also make speculative assets less attractive.

In other words, the dip is more natural market moves than money switching markets.

Nvidia forecast $130 billion in fourth-quarter sales, beating expectations after Jensen Huang’s abrupt departure. Yet markets remained split: global shares slipped 0.1%, while the Nasdaq gained 0.35% early and CoreWeave dropped 5.4%.

BitMEX co-founder Arthur Hayes sees the IPO less as a drain than a barometer. “Bitcoin’s going to go up too” if Anthropic soars, he said, while a weak debut could deepen pressure across risk assets.

Anthropic is scheduled to price Wednesday and start trading Thursday. Before that debut, Bitcoin must hold the same $62,000 area buyers defended during Sunday’s liquidation wave.

#5083. Senators warn GENIUS Act rules could push stablecoin firms toward federal oversight

The lawmakers urged Treasury to extend safe harbor protections to stablecoin issuers in states expected to establish qualifying regulatory frameworks before January 2028.

Six bipartisan senators on Tuesday urged the US Treasury Department to extend proposed regulatory protections to state-supervised stablecoin issuers, warning that proposed GENIUS Act rules could steer firms toward federal licenses.

In a letter to Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, Senators Cynthia Lummis and Kirsten Gillibrand, along with Angela Alsobrooks, Kevin Cramer, Pete Ricketts and Mark Warner, said Treasury’s proposal offers a safe harbor to certain issuers with pending federal applications and regulator-approved waivers, but no equivalent protection for state issuers.

The senators warned that issuers “may be incentivized to seek greater operational clarity under a federal license,” including through the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC), which they said was “the opposite of our intent in drafting this legislation.”

The senators urged Treasury to extend the safe harbor to issuers in states expected to have qualifying frameworks in place before Jan. 18, 2028, reducing uncertainty while certification reviews are pending.

The senators’ request follows a June appeal, urging Treasury to preserve state participation and clarify the certification process.

The GENIUS Act generally allows issuers with $10 billion or less in outstanding stablecoins to operate under qualifying state supervision, with exemptions available above that threshold.

Kevin Wysocki, head of policy at Anchorage Digital, questioned whether a broader safe harbor would make much difference. He said issuers are choosing federal oversight because they risk having to switch regulators if their stablecoins exceed $10 billion in circulation, although exemptions are available.

#5082. Standard Chartered and OCBC test dollar payments in minutes without traditional banking cutoffs

The bank says eligible clients can move funds in near real time, while a separate OCBC and StraitsX trial tested payments between banks.

Standard Chartered said Nov. 19 that eligible clients can now transfer tokenized deposits, bank balances recorded on a digital ledger, between Singapore and Hong Kong in near real time.

That gives corporate treasury teams a way to shift cash between entities in the same group on the bank’s own blockchain.

Meanwhile, a separate trial with OCBC and StraitsX tested payments across two banks’ networks.

The bank’s Singapore expansion covers treasury and investment transactions that use commercial bank deposits. Standard Chartered listed the Singapore dollar, Hong Kong dollar, US dollar, euro, offshore Chinese yuan and British pound among the supported currencies.

It named corporate treasurers, banks, investment platforms, and digital asset businesses as potential users, but detailed eligibility criteria and operating hours for the route remain unspecified.

The bank disclosed round-the-clock settlement when it launched its Hong Kong service Nov. 2. That schedule applies to tokenized deposit transactions settled between participating banks in central bank money through the Hong Kong Monetary Authority’s EnsembleTX infrastructure.

On Nov. 19, Standard Chartered, Singapore bank OCBC and payment infrastructure provider StraitsX said they had completed a controlled trial under the Monetary Authority of Singapore’s BLOOM initiative.

The trial completed US dollar payments across the banks’ separate deposit networks outside conventional cutoffs. Participants said the test showed how payments could move within minutes, including compliance checks, liquidity checks, and settlement.

The trial partners, StraitsX, OCBC and Standard Chartered, explain that each tokenized deposit remains a claim on the bank that issued it. Banks can use different platforms and standards for their deposits, so interbank payments need a way to coordinate settlement across those systems.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

Standard Chartered opened a transfer route for eligible clients between Singapore and Hong Kong. A separate trial tested US dollar payments between two banks’ deposit networks.

A payment across two deposit networks needs a way to coordinate settlement. Each deposit remains a claim on its issuing bank.

Standard Chartered’s release gives no schedule for the live route. The trial partners say a commercial service remains subject to approvals and operational assessments.

The diagram compares the live transfer path described by Standard Chartered with the shared settlement arrangement described by StraitsX, OCBC and Standard Chartered. Connectors represent network relationships.

Standard Chartered’s release gives no schedule for the live route. The trial partners say a commercial service remains subject to approvals and operational assessments.

StraitsX coordinated payment instructions and transaction details and helped with screening and settlement. The shared infrastructure handled the payment process across both networks, from initiation to confirmation.

The partners reported that this setup reduced the need for bilateral prefunding, where banks set aside money in advance to cover payments.

A commercial service based on the trial would still depend on regulatory engagement, participant approval, legal documentation, and operational risk assessments, the partners said. They also flagged settlement finality, meaning when a payment becomes irrevocable.

Banks would need to work out who is responsible for each step, how to screen for financial crime and how to keep enough funds available for payments to settle.

#5081. Strategy (MSTR) Shares Tumble for Sixth Straight Day to $86 as Bitcoin Slides

Strategy (MSTR) shares fell 4.6% on Thursday to close at $86.13, their sixth straight daily decline, as bitcoin dipped below $62,000 and a broad selloff hit U.S. stocks. In after hours trading, shares were down to $85.50.

The stock opened near $93 and slid through the afternoon, finishing near its low of about $86. The Dow Jones Industrial Average dropped more than 1,000 points, and the Nasdaq Composite lost about 2.1%. Bitcoin touched a low of $61,658 during the session, its weakest price since July. It was back near $62,000 late in the afternoon.

The drop adds to a brutal month for the largest corporate holder of bitcoin. Strategy shares are down about 37% from their $136.02 close on Oct. 19, and down about 29% from the $121.07 close on Nov. 10, which is when the latest leg lower began. Thursday was the stock’s sixth consecutive losing session.

The slide has wiped out most of the year’s value. Strategy closed 2025 at $151.95, which puts the stock down about 43% in 2026. The shares fell below $100 on Tuesday and now trade about 11% below Tuesday’s $96.50 close. That day they also dipped under the company’s STRC preferred for the first time since mid-August.

Thursday’s move tracked bitcoin closely. Institutions have kept buying even so, and a New York state pension fund boosted its stake by more than 10% in a recent filing. The bitcoin price slumped under $62,000 as traders weighed the risk of Federal Reserve rate hikes and a sharp pullback in technology stocks.

Strategy added 485 bitcoin in the week ending Nov. 15, according to its Nov. 16 Form 8-K. It paid $33.4 million, or an average of $68,887 per coin, and its total reached 850,849 BTC.

Those coins cost $64.20 billion, or an average of $75,452 each. With bitcoin near $62,000, the stack is worth roughly $52.8 billion, which leaves Strategy with an unrealized loss of about $11 billion. Bitcoin is trading about 18% below the company’s average purchase price.

The 850,849 coins are more than 4% of bitcoin’s 21 million supply cap, and the company also held about $4.9 billion in U.S. dollar reserves as of Nov. 15, which gives it a cushion for preferred dividends and buybacks. Strategy has not announced a new purchase since that filing. It has reported its buys on Mondays in recent weeks, so the next update would normally come Monday.

Most of Strategy’s recent cash has gone to its preferred stock instead of bitcoin. In the same filing, the company said it repurchased 2,282,510 shares of its STRC preferred for $228.1 million, nearly seven times what it spent on bitcoin.

It funded the purchases with $244.7 million raised from selling common stock. The 8-K shows $332.2 million remaining under the preferred repurchase program and $1.0 billion under its common stock buyback authorization.

Executive Chairman Michael Saylor said on X that Strategy plans to keep going. He wrote that STRC traded more than $600 million on Wednesday, calling it the only one of 456 U.S.-listed income preferreds to do so this year.

He said the buybacks add liquidity, lower dividend obligations and strengthen the credit. “We intend to keep buying below $100,” Saylor wrote.

#5079. Why CoinShares Just Sold All Its Ethereum and Half Its Solana

Why CoinShares Just Sold All Its Ethereum and Half Its Solana

CoinShares, one of Europe’s largest digital-asset managers, has sold its entire corporate treasury holding of Ethereum and half of its Solana.

It’s a move that might look like a loss of conviction but is really about something more prosaic: funding a stock buyback.

The decision, disclosed Tuesday in the firm’s third-quarter results, freed up $19.3 million, comprising $16.6 million from the Ethereum sale and $2.7 million from offloading half its Solana.

That covers nearly half of the $40 million share-repurchase program the Jersey-based firm’s board approved in October, and the company was explicit that the proceeds are being “reallocating that capital towards share repurchases.”

CEO Jean-Marie Mognetti framed it as capital discipline rather than a bearish call. “We took the same approach to preparing for the share repurchase program,” he said, noting the sales give the company “the flexibility to act when conditions allow.”

In other words, CoinShares is converting idle treasury crypto into dry powder to buy back its own stock, which it can do under an authorization allowing repurchases of up to 25% of shares outstanding over five years.

The timing is notable. The sale lands during a brutal stretch for crypto markets. Bitcoin was trading around $63,200 on Wednesday, down from six-figure levels earlier in the cycle, while Ethereum sat near $1,770 and Solana around $74, both well off their recent highs.

Spot Ethereum ETFs, meanwhile, have bled cash for days, shedding $29.1 million on Nov. 17 to extend a multi-day outflow streak that has turned Decrypt’s ETF sentiment gauge “bearish.”

Those are precisely the risk-off conditions that have battered Bitcoin treasury firms and pressured the broader sector. For a company whose stock is poised to join the Russell indices in December, deploying capital into buybacks at depressed prices is a conventional play, even if the optics of a crypto manager selling crypto invite a second look.

In terms of scale, these were small corporate treasury positions, not client assets. CoinShares’ total assets under management recovered to $7.4 billion by quarter-end, up from $5.5 billion three months earlier, and its core business of digital-asset investment products is untouched by the treasury move.

The firm actually swung to a strong quarter, posting $39.6 million in net income as net inflows accelerated to $125 million and its lending and trading desks rebounded.

So the sale isn’t CoinShares souring on Ethereum or Solana, assets it still manages billions in on behalf of clients. It’s a treasury-management decision by a newly profitable, soon-to-be-index-listed company choosing to return capital to shareholders rather than let a modest crypto position sit on its balance sheet.

#5077. Pony AI’s robotaxi fares surge nearly 400% but its operating losses barely budge

Pony.ai says fares covered the vehicle costs it tracks in Guangzhou and Shenzhen, leaving the profitability of individual rides unclear.

Pony.ai’s robotaxi fare revenue nearly quintupled year over year in the September quarter, while the company-wide operating loss narrowed to $68.7 million from $69.7 million.

In its Nov. 18 filing, Pony.ai said fare revenue rose nearly 400% year over year. Revenue from its broader robotaxi services business grew 165.4% to $17.8 million from $6.7 million, helped by both passenger fares and higher vehicle deliveries to joint deployments in China and overseas.

In a November service update, separate from its September-quarter figures, Pony.ai said the previously separate operating zones in central Guangzhou and Nansha formed one contiguous area of more than 1,000 square kilometers as of Nov. 17.

Riders can use its PonyPilot app to book trips between Nansha and central Guangzhou, where Pony.ai holds a citywide commercial permit. The area it measured in November covers the connected operating zones where the company says those bookings are available.

CEO James Peng said PonyPilot now lets riders book across Nansha, Haizhu, Tianhe, Huangpu and Panyu. He said the wider network had increased trip distances and the average price per ride in Guangzhou.

On the November earnings call, CFO Leo Wang reported that Pony.ai’s fleets in Guangzhou and Shenzhen had earned enough in fares to more than cover the vehicle costs Pony.ai includes in its calculation.

Pony.ai starts with fares after discounts and refunds, then deducts vehicle depreciation, charging, wages for remote and ground staff, cleaning and maintenance, insurance, parking and communications.

Wang said depreciation accounts for about half of those costs. It covers the vehicle, including the battery and the hardware used to drive it autonomously.

The company said premiums per seventh-generation robotaxi fell 19% from a year earlier at renewal, giving no profit margin for an individual ride.
