#5020. Bitcoin Struggles to Hold $64,000 as ETF Outflows Hit $2.9B in November

Bitcoin Struggles to Hold $64,000 as ETF Outflows Hit $2.9B in November

Bitcoin is struggling to hold the $64,000 level as investors continue to pull money from spot Bitcoin ETFs and brace for another interest rate hike from the Federal Reserve.

The leading cryptocurrency was trading at around $63,800 on Thursday, down 1% on the day and 13% over the past week, according to Decrypt data. It has hovered between roughly $63,000 and $65,000 since the weekend, briefly dipping below $63,000 and touching a 24-hour low of $62,068, per CoinGecko data.

"Bitcoin remains under selling pressure," Daniela Hathorn, senior market analyst at Capital.com, told Decrypt, noting that it has fallen roughly 26% from its late-September peak near $87,000. "Elevated Treasury yields, dollar strength and expectations of further Fed tightening are weighing on risk appetite, while the broader technology selloff has added to the pressure."

U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs recorded $306.4 million in net outflows on Wednesday, after $239.9 million on Tuesday, according to SoSoValue data. The funds have seen outflows on nine of the past 10 trading days, with Monday's small $8 million inflow the only exception, and have lost about $2.9 billion so far in November. Their total net assets have fallen to $77.7 billion. Bitcoin miners have added to the selling, with major miners including Riot and MARA selling some of their holdings to fund a shift into AI infrastructure.

Monday's brief inflow "does not signal a market inflection point," Tim Sun, senior researcher at HashKey, told Decrypt. Net short positions in Bitcoin futures held by leveraged funds on CME have fallen to their lowest level since early 2023, and futures basis yields have narrowed, he said, indicating that hedge funds have largely unwound the basis trade that paired spot ETF purchases with short futures. That means the current outflows are mainly driven by investors actively cutting their Bitcoin exposure, Sun said.

"If macro expectations do not improve, investors may shift from buying the dip to selling into rallies, creating additional overhead resistance on subsequent rebounds," he added.

"Recent Bitcoin ETF outflows and leveraged liquidations" have amplified the decline, Hathorn said. "Despite stronger Nvidia earnings, the market has struggled to stage a significant rebound, suggesting confidence remains fragile. A sustained recovery may require renewed ETF inflows and a more supportive interest-rate outlook."

Some traders have also pointed the finger at Anthropic's upcoming IPO for drawing money away from crypto, though analysts have disputed that the listing is draining crypto liquidity, attributing it instead to ETF outflows, forced selling and rising interest rates.

Traders see a 90.7% chance that the Fed will raise rates by a quarter point at its December 9 meeting, and a 9.3% chance of a half-point hike, according to CME FedWatch. The odds of a half-point move have risen from 6.5% a day earlier. Fed Governor Christopher Waller has called for an even bigger, half-point increase, unless policymakers signal another rise in January.

Hathorn said $64,000 is the immediate level to watch, followed by support around $62,000 and the psychological $60,000 mark. A sustained recovery above $66,000 to $68,000 could ease selling pressure, "but the broader trend remains bearish while lower highs persist," she said.

Onchain analytics firm Glassnode said in its weekly report on Tuesday that the crash had pushed investors’ realized losses to levels seen only near past market lows in 2018, 2020, 2022 and early 2023, a sign of capitulation. Bitcoin is now holding a dense block of recent buyers between $62,000 and $66,000, which grew by about 66,000 BTC during the two days of heaviest selling, Glassnode said. But a daily close below that block could set off a fast drop toward the June low of $58,500, and the Realized Price, the average price at which all Bitcoin last moved, at $53,700.

Traders on Decrypt Predict see a 43% chance that Bitcoin will fall to $60,000 at some point before the end of November, and a 12% chance that it will drop as low as $55,000. They give just a 2% chance that it will climb back to $82,500 this month.

There are some signs of support. About 1.1 million BTC, roughly 5.5% of supply, last changed hands at prices between $60,000 and $64,000, and that band has grown by more than 25,000 BTC in recent days, according to Neil Steinhardt, head of Nexo U.S., in a note shared with Decrypt Wednesday. A dense base of holders with costs near current prices "can act as a support zone, though it is not a floor," he wrote.

Bitcoin has also held up better than gold. The amount of gold one Bitcoin buys rose above its 200-day average this week, to about 17.1 ounces, around 40% above its March low, Steinhardt said.

#5018. Singapore settles $77.5M tokenized bill sale with wholesale CBDC

MAS issued the bills directly on its test network, which it plans to rename SG Digital Ledger and use for settlement beyond the trials.

The Monetary Authority of Singapore (MAS) issued 100 million Singapore dollars ($77.5 million) in tokenized bills on Nov. 6, with banks using wholesale central bank digital currency (CBDC) to pay for them.

Five banks submitted bids totaling more than twice the amount on offer, MAS said Thursday. The four-week bills mature on Dec. 4.

MAS issued the bills directly on the Singapore Dollar Test Network. That gives borrowers an asset they could pledge as collateral for loans on the network, the central bank said.

Singapore began experimenting with blockchain-based bank payments in 2016. In 2025, banks used the test network and MAS-issued digital currency to settle overnight loans.

MAS now plans to turn the Singapore Dollar Test Network, where the bills were issued, into a settlement system for financial institutions, Managing Director Chia Der Jiun said Thursday at the Singapore FinTech Festival. The network will be renamed SG Digital Ledger.

“As the use of tokenized assets and digital money grows, trusted settlement on CBDC will be needed,” Chia said. “It underpins settlement finality and confidence, especially for large value settlement.”

In 2027, MAS plans to issue more tokenized bills, bring more financial institutions into the trials and expand the bills’ use as collateral.

Related: Singapore advances asset tokenization with new MAS frameworks

#5015. US launches cybercrime center with TRM Labs to help trace crypto tied to scams

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The Threat Disruption Center will help investigators share information with payments, crypto, and technology companies to disrupt scams.

The Homeland Security Task Force announced a new unit to disrupt criminal networks behind online scams, with losses tied to cryptocurrency fraud reported to the FBI exceeding $11 billion last year.

Dubbed the Threat Disruption Center, the unit comprises more than 30 federal agencies and over 440 state and local partners with shared intelligence and technology resources to thwart cybercrime.

The task force said investigators will share information with participating companies as they trace funds and identify the networks behind the scams. Exchanges and payment providers can stop transfers or freeze funds, while social media companies can take down accounts used to target victims.

Meanwhile, law enforcement will continue its investigations and pursue court-authorized seizures.

"These partnerships will allow us to identify and dismantle criminal networks faster, disrupt fraud at its source, protect victims, and prevent Americans from becoming the next target," said John A. Condon, acting executive associate director of Homeland Security Investigations.

In its 2025 Internet Crime Report, the FBI recorded 181,565 complaints involving cryptocurrency. Crypto investment fraud accounted for $7.2 billion of the more than $11 billion in reported losses.

The bureau said at the time that many of those schemes were operated by criminal networks in Southeast Asia, some of which use trafficked people to run scam operations.

TRM Labs said its Ardence platform will serve as the center’s primary investigative tool, which investigators will use to process victim complaints, trace cryptocurrency transactions, and map criminal networks.

According to government procurement records, the company was awarded a $94.66 million contract in July to support the operation. The agreement runs through Aug. 29, 2027.

Meanwhile, the new center builds on Operation Level Up, the initiative the FBI launched in 2024 to identify victims of cryptocurrency investment fraud and warn them before they lose more money. The operation has notified more than 8,000 victims and helped prevent over $500 million in losses, according to the bureau.

#5012. Bitcoin finance protocol Hashi goes live on Sui mainnet

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Fluid is taking hBTC for USDC loans, and HoneyB has opened one hBTC vault and one USDC vault on Upshift.

Hashi, Sui's (SUI) bitcoin finance protocol, is now live on mainnet, with its hBTC token available for use as collateral in lending and other onchain products.

Hashi holds deposited BTC Bitcoin (BTC) on the Bitcoin network and mints hBTC on Sui 1:1, according to a statement shared with The Block. Sui said Hashi is decentralized by design and secured by validators rather than a single entity, a model used by some wrapped-bitcoin products.

Hashi previously launched on testnet in July. On Oct. 8, Sui added Anchorage Digital to its list of launch partners and said the group had made more than $500 million in capital commitments, initially intending to roll out the mainnet that month.

More than 20 partners are building markets, products, and access around Hashi, according to Sui, including Anchorage Digital, Fluid, HoneyB, and Upshift.

Decentralized lending protocol Fluid is using hBTC as collateral for USDC loans on Sui, with additional borrowing options expected.

"Bitcoin is the largest pool of idle capital in crypto," Samyak Jain, founder and CEO at Fluid, said. "With Hashi, it can now power deep, capital-efficient credit markets."

Bitcoin yield platform HoneyB has opened two vaults on DeFi vault infrastructure provider Upshift, one for USDC and the other for hBTC. Sui said the strategies may include real-world assets, alternative products, and publicly traded assets. Sui-based vault platform Ember Protocol plans to add its Hashi vaults later this year, according to the statement.

"By connecting custodians, exchanges, and DeFi protocols to professionally managed strategies, we're opening Bitcoin finance to a much wider audience," Upshift co-CEO and co-founder Aya Kantorovich said.

"Bitcoin holders have spent years watching their assets appreciate without putting them to work. We're changing that, bringing real income strategies to BTC and stablecoin holders through Sui," Josh Gordon, CEO at HoneyB developer Nectar Group, added.

Nectar Group raised $5 million at a $30 million valuation last month. Gordon told The Block at the time that its first product would be offered to institutional investors outside the U.S. and target annual returns of about 2% to 4% on bitcoin after costs and fees.

Sui named Blueprint Finance (Concrete), Bluefin, Cetus, Current, and Suilend among the DeFi protocols expected to provide retail access to hBTC lending, stablecoin credit, and yield strategies. Institutions can access hBTC and select Fluid strategies through Porto, Anchorage Digital's self-custody wallet. Cumberland and Wave Digital Assets are expected to provide BTC and stablecoin liquidity, while BitGo access and tri-party settlement are due in early 2027.

Fenwick attorneys argued in an April analysis that Hashi's deposit and redemption mechanics should not constitute taxable events under U.S. federal income tax law, Sui said.

Sui also supports tBTC. Threshold Network added Sui to the networks supported by its tBTC vault tools last November, alongside Ethereum, Arbitrum, Base, and Polygon.

#5011. Robert Pattinson’s James Bond Needs a Killer Cast. Here’s Who Should Join Him.

A great 007 movie isn’t just about the man himself. Now we can start to find the next Bond Girl, villain, and more—and really have some fun.

OUR LONG INTERNATIONAL nightmare is finally over: We have a new James Bond. On Monday, Robert Pattinson was announced as 007, beating out contenders that included Callum Turner, Jack Lowden, and Josh O’Connor. Considering Pattinson is currently double dipping as Bruce Wayne—and the fact that Bond is historically a star-making role, rather than a role for an established star—his casting won’t be everyone’s cup of tea. (My two cents: he would’ve been a god-tier Bond villain.)

Still, it’s hard to deny that Pattinson is going to crush the role. From buzzy indies like The Lighthouse and High Life to blockbusters like Mickey 17 and The Odyssey, he’s long been on a heater. (Nobody using Edward Cullen as a punchline against him is further proof of that.) For Amazon, whose stewardship of the Bond franchise was always going to be met with scrutiny, there’s no shame in making the safe, sensible choice.

But landing on a new 007 is only the beginning. James Bond isn’t just about the title character: The franchise can also be a career-defining move for actors enlisted to play recurring characters (M) and archetypes (the gimmicky henchmen) that bring the world to life. For Pattinson and director Denis Villeneuve, this new Bond entry needs to be populated with the very best supporting players Jeff Bezos’s money can buy.

With that in mind, here’s our dream casting for the seven most important non-Bond roles in the new film.

The Bond franchise has been around for decades, and the films are often a reflection of both the world around them and the current state of moviemaking. The Timothy Dalton era was gritty and grounded as the Cold War wound down; the Daniel Craig movies were serialized in the age of cinematic universes. For a modern-day Bond, then, we should have a villain that feels made for the 2020s. In other words: cast a sinister nerd—and more specifically, cast Samuel Blenkin.

In biopics and documentaries alike, tech bros are cinema’s latest villain du jour. Between the untold economic and environmental damage they've caused in the real world, nobody is going to feel bad about these guys being painted in an unflattering light. In that respect, Blenkin would be a fun casting. If Tenet was a great test run for Pattinson as 007, then Alien: Earth, in which Blenkin plays an impetuous tech bro with the gall to mess around with Xenomorphs (!!!), is proof that he could bring the same (respectfully) punchable energy to Bond. Say what you will about Zack Snyder’s DC movies, but picking the actor who played Mark Zuckerberg to be his Lex Luthor was ahead of the curve. Bond would be wise to meet the moment.

Runner-up: Jensen Huang (with the leather jacket, obviously).

The next M has big shoes to fill. Dame Judi Dench played the character across eight films and two separate Bonds over a period of 20 years, and Ralph Fiennes then made the role his own (as he always does), taking over the mantle for Daniel Craig’s final three films.

But sometimes, the obvious pick is the right one. Emma Thompson is acting royalty with a commanding air of authority, which is exactly what M calls for. I can just as easily picture her chastising Bond for a reckless bit of espionage as I can see her delivering some characteristically bone-dry British wit. Imagine how fucking electric a Pattinson-Thompson tête-à-tête would be.

Runner-up: Kristin Scott Thomas or Fiona Shaw.

We haven’t had an American Bond Girl since Halle Berry in Die Another Day, a film that, unfortunately, doesn’t hold up on rewatch on account of [gestures at Pierce Brosnan surfing a CGI tsunami]. Let’s have Bond dip back stateside with one of Hollywood’s most exciting young stars—and one whose name already sounds like that of a Bond Girl—in Chase Infiniti. Her resume is small but impressive: the emotional center of Paul Thomas Anderson’s Best Picture-winning One Battle After Another, a supporting role in Apple TV’s Presumed Innocent, and the lead of the Handmaid’s Tale spinoff The Testaments. And if Infiniti’s on-screen work isn’t enough to move the needle, she’s rocked enough red carpet looks to prove she knows how to make an eye-popping entrance. For a Bond Girl, that’s half the battle.

Runner-up: Margaret Qualley, mostly because she basically auditioned to be a Bond Girl at the 97th Academy Awards’ Bond tribute.

Aimee Lou Wood’s vibe as a performer is, in a word, cute. In her most notable performances—The White Lotus and Sex Education chief among them—there’s a natural warmth and mischievousness that would make her a fun scene partner for Pattinson’s Bond. Moneypenny is traditionally the one person at MI6 who can get flirty with Bond without worrying about getting a lecture from M after the fact. Wood has the charm and comic timing to make that dynamic sing.

Runner-up if Moneypenny becomes Irish: Nicola Coughlan.

Pattinson, Anne Hathaway, and (quite deservedly) Samantha Morton have been receiving much of the praise for their work in The Odyssey, but Himesh Patel’s beleaguered Eurylochus might be the film’s unsung hero. Now, he can pivot to playing the Bond franchise’s underappreciated MVP in Q: a perpetually put-upon tech genius who has to watch a reckless womanizer repeatedly destroy million-dollar prototypes. Patel has the dry, exasperated delivery down pat, and he’d be a perfect straight man for Pattinson’s particular brand of chaos (and it would mark a more-than-welcome Tenet reunion). Perhaps Q will belatedly give Patel the accolades he deserved for The Odyssey. (Also known as the Rhea Seehorn Principle.)

Runner-up: Matt Berry in character as Laszlo Cravensworth.

This might be recency bias, because I just watched Kelvin Harrison Jr. in The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping (say that three times fast). Harrison Jr. is Beetee Latier in the prequel, who was previously played by none other than … Jeffrey Wright. Credit where it’s due: Harrison Jr. does a mean Jeffrey Wright impression in the film.

However, we don’t need to see Harrison Jr. reheat his Wright impression in the new Bond. What I’ve admired in everything I’ve seen him in is how chameleonic he can be—to the extent that you could conceivably cast him in just about any role and he’d find a way to make it his own. (There’s no better evidence that he could play a killer spy than his performance in the psychological thriller Luce, where his character is eerily hard to get a read on.) I want a fresh take on Leiter, and the one thing we can say for Harrison Jr.: he’ll deliver on that promise.

Runner-up: John David Washington for peak Tenet vibes.

Look, I get it: Do we really need Tormund Giantsbane 2.0 in a Bond movie? But Hivju doesn’t deserve to be defined by that role for the rest of his career. He’s got the kind of outsized screen presence that demands an at-bat as a Bond henchman. (You can totally picture him getting excited by Bond drawing blood during a fight.) Sure, Villeneuve isn’t exactly known for humor in his projects, but Bond films have a long tradition of colorful henchmen, and Hivju could dial up the menace without taking himself too seriously. Give him a gimmick, a tweed suit, and no more than four lines of dialogue, and I promise that Hivju will steal every scene he’s in.

Runner-up: Olivier Richters for that Reacher season 3 fight scene alone.

Miles Surrey is a Brooklyn-based culture writer who covers television, film, and whatever your dad is interested in. His work can also be found at The Ringer, Vox, Vice, and The A.V. Club.

#5010. GameStop CEO Says Digital-Only Games Have ‘Hurt Consumers’ as GTA 6 Skips Discs

GameStop CEO Says Digital-Only Games Have ‘Hurt Consumers’ as GTA 6 Skips Discs

GameStop CEO Ryan Cohen has criticized video game publishers for abandoning physical discs, as Grand Theft Auto 6, one of the most anticipated games ever made, launches on Thursday as a digital download only.

"When you buy a game, you should be able to sell it, trade it or lend it to a friend," Cohen told Bloomberg. "Unquestionably, the decision to move to all digital has hurt consumers, and it's also hurt GameStop."

Cohen declined to name specific companies, but Take-Two Interactive is releasing the $80 game only as a download, with retailers selling boxes containing download codes. Asked about presales, Cohen said: "It's a code in a box. I mean, we're selling a lot of it."

Take-Two declined to comment to Bloomberg.

The shift hits a core part of GameStop's business. Pre-owned and refurbished products made up 22% of the retailer's sales, about $171 million, in the quarter that ended August 1, down 32% from a year earlier, according to Bloomberg.

Cohen said he has spoken with publishers, who he argued benefit from limiting trade-ins because used copies compete with new sales. "They're taking away competition," he said.

Sony announced in July that it will stop producing physical discs for new PlayStation games in January 2028, a move experts have said will hit low-income gamers hardest. PlayStation founder Ken Kutaragi has dismissed the backlash as a "mentality issue," while an Infinity Ward veteran said publishers have pushed to cut discs since 2007.

Cohen said he has met with Rep. Ritchie Torres (D-NY), who is backing the Power to the Players Act, which would require gaming companies to give consumers ongoing access to the titles they buy. "A game that can be taken away the moment a company pulls the plug is not a purchase. It is a rental disguised as a sale," Torres said in October.

In Europe, the Stop Killing Games campaign gathered 1.3 million signatures for a European Citizens' Initiative calling for publishers to keep games playable after support ends.

The question of whether buyers own digital games has also reached the courts. Sony has argued in court that digital games can't be owned, prompting some in the crypto industry to pitch blockchain-based ownership as a fix.

Cohen's GameStop has also made bets beyond gaming. The company bought 4,710 BTC for more than $500 million in 2025, and in March moved all but one of them into a covered call strategy on Coinbase Prime to generate income. In May, after eBay rejected its takeover offer, GameStop sought shareholder approval to raise its authorized share count to 2.5 billion as it built a stake in the online marketplace.

#5008. Hill, Moreno blame delays for missed Clarity Act window but differ on reach of agency rulemaking

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Moreno says agencies can deliver 80% of a crypto framework, while Hill warns rulemaking cannot replace lasting legislation.

Rep. French Hill and Sen. Bernie Moreno said Congress missed a chance to pass the Clarity Act while momentum from the GENIUS Act was strong, but offered different assessments of how much regulators can accomplish while market structure legislation remains stalled.

Speaking in separate interviews on Thursday at The Hill and The Block's Capitol Crypto Forum, Hill argued that agency action cannot provide the permanent legal certainty that legislation would. Moreno said regulators could deliver much of the framework themselves, though he acknowledged legislation is the ideal route.

"I’m a little bit more bullish than French," Moreno said. "I think it can accomplish 80% through a very rigorous rulemaking process that can’t be unwound."

The Ohio senator added that the Securities and Exchange Commission and Commodity Futures Trading Commission should use the next two years to establish rules that would make it difficult for a future administration to undo.

"Is that the right way to do it? No," he added. "I think the right way to do it is Congress should pass the Clarity Act."

Hill, the Arkansas Republican who also chairs the House Financial Services Committee, said since the Clarity Act's failure, regulators have mostly followed in the footsteps and goals of market structure proposals. But he added that their authority is limited.

"My argument is it’s not a permanent fix. It is subject to litigation. It does not have the force of law," Hill said.

Both Hill and Moreno said it was a mistake to advance the GENIUS Act separately from Clarity, which passed the House but stalled out after a failed Senate vote in September.

"I urged the Senate and the administration last summer to have Clarity and GENIUS passed together in the House," Hill said. "And I still stand by that."

Moreno, a former car dealer, gave a sales analogy for the breakdown.

"Time kills all deals," he said, adding that lawmakers failed to capitalize on momentum from the stablecoin legislation.

"The bill started at 300 pages, ended up at 600 pages," Moreno said.

Neither had much hope for passage during the lame-duck session. Asked what was realistically possible to achieve over the coming weeks, Moreno answered: "Zero. Zero."

Hill was slightly more optimistic, saying lawmakers "never give up hope in a lame duck," though "the chances are not high."

Hill said it was a good sign that Rep. Maxine Waters pointed out digital asset market structure as an issue she intended to address if she's elected as committee chair.

Moreno also said bipartisan negotiations were not off the table, while blaming earlier talks for being "tornadoed" in disputes over President Donald Trump and ethics.

He said he would consider restarting negotiations with the House-passed legislation.

"By the time we got to September with the Senate version, it was just very, very, very complicated," Moreno said.

Hill argued that Congress still needs to finish the job: "To have GENIUS but not market structure is a huge mistake."

#5005. Tokenized Stocks Shrug Off the Crypto Selloff

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Sign up to get 2 days early access to each edition of Tokenization Briefing

Bitcoin fell as low as $62k late Saturday, its sixth straight weekly decline. Meanwhile, tokenized stocks crossed $4 billion in onchain value for the first time on Nov 11. Tokenized stocks are now roughly five times their value on January 1.

Tokenized stocks track equity prices, not crypto, so a falling BTC doesn’t directly pull their prices down. What stands out, however, is that crypto users appear to keep buying them through the selloff. Demand for 24/7 trading and onchain access to US equities appears to have found strong product-market fit as they are holding up even as the rest of the crypto market weakens.

In September, rising share prices drove most of the growth in value of tokenized stocks. Since mid-October, new minting has driven the growth in value even as share prices dropped. Over the eleven weeks to Nov 13, minting added $1.12 billion while price moves took away $339 million. Binance’s bStocks accounted for about four fifths of that minting, led by NVIDIA, SpaceX and Circle.

Market value has stalled over the past two weeks. We expect minting to pick back up again with the launch of OKXICE on Nov 23 opening large-cap tokenized stocks to US retail.

Securitize reported $11.5 million in tokenization revenue in Q3, its highest on record and 28% more than Q3 2025. Securitize's average tokenized assets reached a record $5 billion as of Q3 as BlackRock, Ark Invest and Neuberger brought new products onchain.

Tokenization revenue is more variable because it rises and falls with issuance, while asset servicing has settled around $7 million a quarter. Securitize's acquisition of River One could help smooth out those revenue swings and diversify its top line. Lending against tokenized assets adds recurring income on top of issuance. We expect credit to show up as Securitize's third revenue line within a year of the deal closing.

Most of the roughly $4 billion of tokenized stock sits in wrappers that US investors can't buy. Only about 10% is open to American investors, and almost all of that is crypto companies including Securitize, Figure, Exodus and Forward Industries. NVIDIA, SpaceX and the other large names are effectively offshore only.

That could change on Nov 23, when OKXICE opens to US customers who have onboarded through its CeDeFi Wallet. It’s the first venue built for American retail at scale, so the onshore share should grow post-launch. We expect issuer-backed listings to drive it, with 67 companies having opted out.

#5003. IOSCO warns quantum upgrades could slow blockchains, leave security gaps

A Solana test network ran about 90% slower with quantum-resistant signatures, and wallets that keep using older keys could remain vulnerable.

The International Organization of Securities Commissions (IOSCO) warned that upgrading blockchains to withstand quantum attacks could slow transactions and raise fees.

In a report released Thursday, IOSCO said some quantum-resistant digital signatures take up far more space than those used to authorize transactions today. Larger signatures mean fewer transactions can fit in each block, which could increase congestion, push up fees and require more storage.

IOSCO cited an experiment in which a test version of Solana ran about 90% slower with quantum-resistant signatures.

No known quantum computer can break the cryptography that secures these networks today. IOSCO urged firms to start preparing now because the transition could take years.

IOSCO said layer-1 networks use cryptography both to authorize transactions and to reach agreement on which blocks to add. Even after an upgrade, funds in dormant wallets and applications could remain vulnerable if they still rely on older keys.

Layer-2 networks can use quantum-resistant proofs to verify transactions and still depend on vulnerable systems to settle those transactions, move assets between chains or approve upgrades.

In April, Bitcoin advocate Samson Mow warned that larger post-quantum signatures could reduce Bitcoin’s throughput and that rushed fixes might introduce new vulnerabilities.

In October, Starknet’s community backed research into becoming an independent, quantum-resistant layer-1 network, following concerns over its reliance on Ethereum’s security upgrade timeline.

Ethereum researcher Justin Drake has since outlined a 2028 post-quantum transition target, ahead of the network’s existing 2029 roadmap.
